I do not, finally, use AI tools to produce work in the disciplines where my own craft is the value I am bringing to the client. The Continuum frame from Chapter Three is, for me, a daily operational practice, not a theoretical model. The places I sit on the right edge of the line are deliberately chosen. The places I sit on the left are deliberately defended.

[LONG PAUSE]

The complete toolchain. A categorised reference.

[PAUSE]

This section is a reference inventory, not a recommendation list. It catalogues every tool, model, platform, app, plugin, LoRA, workflow and service that Dream Machine tracked across its thirty-two issues, from October twenty twenty-five to June twenty twenty-six. Some are dominant. Some are niche. Some have already been bought, renamed or discontinued by the time you read this. The point of the list is not "what to use." The point is what existed, in this period, in the creative-AI toolchain — so that the shape of the field is on the record.

[PAUSE]

A word on the list's grain. I have tried to err on the side of inclusion. Where a single company ships multiple closely-related products — Adobe's Sneaks portfolio, the Runway Gen Four Point Five family, the Qwen-Edit LoRA series — I have grouped them under the parent entry but called out the constituent tools. Because in this period each constituent shipped to working creatives separately and changed at its own cadence. Where a tool was a one-issue demo I could not later verify, I have still listed it. That the demo existed at all is part of the field's history. Where a tool's name conflicts with another — there are at least three things called "Wonder" in the period the book covers — I have annotated.

[PAUSE]

The list runs to roughly six hundred entries. Skim it. Use the categories. Come back to specific sections when you need them.

[LONG PAUSE]

Foundation models and Large Language Models.

[PAUSE]

The foundation-model layer begins with ChatGPT, G.P.T. Five and G.P.T. Five Pro from Open A.I. — the dominant consumer L.L.M. (Large Language Model) and reference foundation model, eight hundred to nine hundred million weekly active users, G.P.T. Five and G.P.T. Five Pro announced at DevDay twenty twenty-five.

[PAUSE]

Claude, Claude Code, Claude Apps and Claude Skills, from Anthropic. The writers' and developers' favoured second. Strong long-context performance. The agentic coding environment that underlies Sony's forty-nine-agent, seventy-two-skill stack. Claude for Legal launched May twenty twenty-six.

[PAUSE]

Gemini, Gemini Two Point Five Flash, Gemini Three and Gemini Three Point One Flash, from Google. Google's multimodal L.L.M. family. Desktop users growing one hundred and fifty-five percent year-on-year in twenty twenty-five to twenty twenty-six. Gemini Three Point One Flash T-T-S is the most controllable Google voice model as of spring twenty twenty-six.

[PAUSE]

Llama, from Meta — the dominant open-weight foundation model.

[PAUSE]

Mistral, with Mistral Voxtral and Mistral Transcribe Two — European open-source L.L.M. Voxtral is the next-generation speech-to-text family.

[PAUSE]

Qwen, and Qwen Three Point Five Omni, from Alibaba. Chinese open-source L.L.M., with image, video and audio variants. Omni is the multimodal family covering text, images, audio and video.

[PAUSE]

DeepSeek — the Chinese open-source L.L.M. used heavily in agentic stacks.

[PAUSE]

Phi and Phi Mini, from Microsoft — lightweight foundation models.

[PAUSE]

Lyria, Lyria Three and Lyria Three Pro, from Google DeepMind. Text-to-music foundation model family. Lyria Three ships with SynthID watermarking and the Lyria Camera interactive demo.

[PAUSE]

Grok, from xAI. xAI's L.L.M. family, surfacing in the creative stack through Grok Imagine. Voice cloning via the xAI A.P.I. from May twenty twenty-six.

[PAUSE]

Cohere Transcribe — enterprise transcription model. Thirty-three hours of audio in twelve minutes.

[PAUSE]

And Microsoft VibeVoice — open-source frontier voice AI model.

[LONG PAUSE]

AI video models.

[PAUSE]

Sora and Sora Two, from Open A.I. — the model that opened the period. Physical realism, audio integration, multi-shot world-state persistence. iOS app hit one million downloads in five days. Sora Two Character Creation surfaced on fal in March twenty twenty-six.

[PAUSE]

Veo Three, Veo Three Point One, Veo Three Point One Ingredients to Video, and Veo Three Point One Lite, from Google DeepMind. The working filmmaker's preferred model for cinematic control. Ingredients to Video shipped to YouTube Shorts and YouTube Create. Veo Three Point One Lite is the lower-cost text- and image-to-video tier.

[PAUSE]

Runway Gen Four, Gen Four Point Five, Gen Four Point Five Image-to-Video, Workflows, Story Panels, Characters A.P.I., Ad Concepter, Apps for Advertising, and Aleph Two Point Zero. The most-integrated commercial AI-video stack. Story Panels generate three-panel storyboards from a single image. Characters A.P.I. ships real-time intelligent avatars. The Vera Rubin-powered real-time model runs under one hundred milliseconds latency. Aleph Two Point Zero, in June twenty twenty-six, lets the user edit a single frame in a video and propagates that edit consistently across the rest of the clip. The cleanest frame-as-handle video-editing pattern yet shipped.

[PAUSE]

Kling, Kling Two Point Five Turbo, Kling O-One, Kling Two Point Six, Kling Three Point Zero, Kling X-Dub, and Kling Motion Control Three Point Zero, from Kuaishou. Strong on physics and trajectory control. Three Point Zero adds multi-shot control, multilingual audio and 4K image generation. X-Dub is the context-rich visual dubbing variant.

[PAUSE]

Pika Two Point Zero, and PikaStream One Point Zero. Iteration-speed-focused video generation. PikaStream brings AI agents into live video calls.

[PAUSE]

Luma Dream Machine, UNI-One, UNI-One Point One, Ray3 Modify, and Luma Dream Brief. Luma's video, world-and-reasoning, and modification stack. UNI-One Point One ships with prompt enhancement and built-in research. Dream Brief is the one million dollar Cannes Lions competition.

[PAUSE]

Wan Two Point Two, Wan Two Point Five, and Wan Two Point Six, from Alibaba Qwen. Camera-controlled video generation. Two Point Six adds character reference and multishot capabilities.

[PAUSE]

Hunyuan Video and Hunyuan Image-to-Video, from Tencent — open-source video model.

[PAUSE]

Seedance Two Point Zero, SeeDream Four and SeeDream Four Point Five, from ByteDance. Image-to-video and finished-video models, integrated into CapCut, Dreamina and Freepik. Per-second cost fell below fourteen cents by March twenty twenty-six.

[PAUSE]

Higgsfield, Higgsfield Sketch-to-Video, Higgsfield Popcorn, Higgsfield Relight, Higgsfield Shots, WAN Camera Control, Higgsfield Adobe Premiere Plugin, and Higgsfield After Effects Plugin. The social-media-marketer video platform. Eighty million dollars raised at one point three billion dollar valuation, two hundred million dollars revenue in nine months. Shots produces multiple storyboard images from a single shot. The Adobe Premiere Pro and After Effects plugins in June twenty twenty-six bring Higgsfield generation directly into the professional editorial workflow.

[PAUSE]

Bernini, from ByteDance — an AI video generation and editing framework. A complementary video-editing layer to ByteDance's Seedance generation stack.

[PAUSE]

LTX-Two, LTX-Two Point Three, LTX-Two Point Three Colorizer, LTX-H-D-R, LTX Studio, LTX-Two Audio-to-Video, LTX-Two Lip Sync, and LTX-Two Real-Time. Open-source video generation with audio sync. LTX-Two Point Three is high-resolution, fast, cinematic, with native lip-sync. LTX H-D-R, in beta, ships H-D-R processing.

AI image models and tools.

[PAUSE]

Midjourney, and Midjourney V Eight Point One — the aesthetic-leadership product. Discord and X-native. V Eight Point One ships native 2K H-D rendering at three times the speed and three times the cost reduction.

Flux, Flux Two, Flux Two Max, and Flux Two klein, from Black Forest Labs. Open-weight, fine-control, the open-source default through twenty twenty-five and twenty twenty-six. klein is the four-billion-parameter lightweight model.

Adobe Firefly, Firefly Image Model Five, Firefly Foundry, Firefly Boards, and Firefly Precision Flow. Image Model Five. Foundry for custom corporate training. Firefly Boards for moodboards. Precision Flow for granular AI editing control, in beta. Forty-five percent of Creative Cloud users active. Twenty-two billion-plus assets generated by April twenty twenty-five.

Stable Diffusion, Stable Diffusion Three, and Stable-Layers, from Stability AI. The foundational open-source image model. Stable-Layers, in June twenty twenty-six, splits generated images into editable compositional layers. A structural shift from regeneration to post-generation editing.

Ideogram Four Point Zero — fourth-generation image model. Open weights, fine-tuneable on own data, runs on own hardware. Available on every Ideogram plan and via A.P.I.

Qwen-Image-Twenty-Five-Twelve, Qwen-Image-Edit-Twenty-Five-Eleven, Qwen Twenty-Five-Eleven Time Travel, and Qwen-Edit Twenty-Five-Oh-Nine. The dominant open-source image-editing model family. I.P. licensing, governance and behavioural-licence platforms.

[PAUSE]

The contract-and-governance layer that emerged through twenty twenty-five and twenty twenty-six. The deep analytical treatment of how these models work — the three Cs of consent, credit, compensation, the five-model comparison, the corrected Disney and Open A.I. record, the transaction-cost reframe — is in Appendix L, The Programmable Brand.

[PAUSE]

Hasbro Sixth Wall and CharacterOS — in-house AI studio launched the third of June twenty twenty-six. To license the behaviour of Hasbro characters — Optimus Prime, Megatron, Cobra Commander, the Clue cast — under a runtime governance layer that encodes canon, voice, personality, lore and safety limits. Business-to-Business programmatic licence with a talent-participation pay-out structure for original performers. Initial twelve-character catalogue. Thirteen-plus and enterprise focus.

[PAUSE]

Verified by Spotify, and the Spotify–U-M-G fan-remix product. Spotify's Verified by Spotify badge and the opt-in catalogue infrastructure underneath the May twenty twenty-six Spotify and Universal Music Group landmark licensing agreement for fan-made AI covers and remixes. Consent, credit, compensation framework. Paid Premium add-on. New royalty stream to artists and songwriters.

[PAUSE]

Disney and Open A.I. licensing framework — announced the eleventh of December twenty twenty-five, never executed. The one billion dollar licensing announcement that became the reference contract architecture for legacy-I.P. and frontier-AI deals despite the deal itself collapsing when Open A.I. shut down Sora on the twenty-fourth of March twenty twenty-six. I.P.-not-training, talent-voice-and-likeness excluded, jointly-governed steering committee, equity-and-warrants structure. The framework survived the platform's death.

[PAUSE]

Elf.tech and GrimesAI — from CreateSafe and Triniti A.P.I. Grimes's open-voice platform. Upload your vocal, get back a synthesised GrimesAI performance, release commercially with a roughly fifty percent master-royalty split. Analogised by her team to fan-fiction and fan-art cultures. Around fifteen thousand tracks generated under the licence.

[PAUSE]

Holly Plus, and the Holly Herndon DAO — public digital twin of the artist's voice wrapped in collective DAO governance for commercial-use decisions and the distribution of proceeds. Paired with the Have I Been Trained, from Spawning, consent-layer tooling.

[PAUSE]

Eleven Labs Heritage Voices, and Iconic Marketplace — with CMG Worldwide and partners. Heritage-voice licensing pipeline confined to a single text-to-speech Reader app, walled off from the general voice-cloning database. Voices include Judy Garland, James Dean, Burt Reynolds, Laurence Olivier, the Stan Lee estate and Michael Caine.

[PAUSE]

Meta Celebrity Chatbots, from twenty twenty-three — the closed-static likeness-rental anti-pattern referenced for contrast in Appendix L. High flat fee — reportedly up to roughly five million dollars per celebrity over two years — for platform-owned, frozen personas with no participatory layer for users or the wider creator community.

[PAUSE]

And the Cannes AI Disclosure Standard — industry-coordination labelling standard for production-side AI use. The inter-industry analogue of the per-platform governance layers above.

[PAUSE]

This is the catalogue. By the time you read it, it will be incomplete. New tools have shipped. Some on this list have been bought, renamed or killed. Treat it as a snapshot of one year of toolchain at the moment the toolchain became a stack rather than a list. And use it to orient yourself in whatever the state of play is when you pick the book up.

[PAUSE]

For a deeper analytical treatment of the adoption telemetry behind many of these tools — Firefly's twenty-two-billion-asset growth curve, ChatGPT's eight hundred to nine hundred million weekly active user figures, the Veo and Sora professional split, the GDC sentiment-versus-usage divergence — see Appendix E, Dynamics of Generative AI Adoption.

[LONG PAUSE]

How to build a toolchain you can defend.

[PAUSE]

The last thing I want to say in this chapter is something I have said in talks more often than anything else, because working creatives ask me this question, in some variant, every week.

[PAUSE]

How do I decide what tools to use, in a market that is changing this fast, without burning my whole month re-learning interfaces?

[PAUSE]

My short answer is — build the toolchain in layers, and accept that the layers move at different speeds.

[PAUSE]

The bottom layer — your foundation model, your modality stack, your agent platform — will change frequently. Treat it as ephemeral. Pick the best tools available this quarter and be ready to swap them next quarter.

[PAUSE]

The middle layer — your creative software. Adobe, Unreal, Davinci, Pro Tools, Blender, Logic — will change more slowly, and is the layer in which the AI capability will be progressively absorbed. Treat it as the long-term home of your craft. Learn it deeply.

[PAUSE]

The top layer — your judgement, your taste, your briefing skill, your integration sense — does not change. It is the layer the agents cannot copy, the layer the platforms cannot ship, and the layer the next model release does not depreciate. Spend more time here than the toolchain wants you to.

[PAUSE]

The mistake I see working creatives make most often is to over-optimise the bottom layer and under-invest the top. The platform companies want you to spend your working hours chasing the new model release. The work that pays the bills, the work that finds an audience, and the work that survives a transition is built on the layer the platform companies cannot reach.

[PAUSE]

[calm] [EMPHASIS] The toolchain, in the end, is the means. The work is the end. The tools change. The work, if it is any good, lasts.

[PAUSE]

That is the working operating model my studio has run on for the period this book covers. It is the model I would commend to anyone building, this year or next, a creative practice that survives the rest of the decade.

[PAUSE]

[calm] The transition is going to keep going. The tools will keep changing. The work that matters, on the other side, will be made by the people who kept their attention on the right layer.

[LONG PAUSE]

END OF CHAPTER

[LONG PAUSE]


CHAPTER SEVENTEEN

Five Years Inside the Dream Machine.

[PAUSE]

I called the newsletter Dream Machine in late September twenty twenty-five because the phrase caught something I couldn't yet articulate. I have said elsewhere in this book — in Chapter Fifteen, and in the front matter — what I think the phrase has come to mean over the period the book covers. An apparatus capable of producing what until recently required a human mind, amplifying and distributing the dreaming of the humans who direct it.

[PAUSE]

What I have not done, anywhere else in the book, is let myself off the leash about what the apparatus might become. I have been careful, chapter by chapter, to put the evidence in front of the argument. The predictions in Chapter Fifteen are dated, falsifiable, defensive — written so the reader picking up the book in twenty thirty can grade them with a stopwatch.

[PAUSE]

[hesitant] This chapter is the opposite. This is the chapter where I let myself imagine what the next five years might look like if the trajectory of the previous eight months extends, accelerates, breaks and recombines in the ways I half-suspect it will but cannot, in any conventional analytical register, prove.

[PAUSE]

I want to be honest about the rules I am giving myself for this chapter, because they are looser than the rules the rest of the book has run on.

[PAUSE]

The rule is. Each scenario has to be argued from something already in the book — a chapter, a number, a framework, a quoted source. I am not allowed to introduce a new mechanism out of thin air. But I am allowed to take a mechanism that is, in spring twenty twenty-six, present in small or early form, and ask what it looks like by twenty thirty-one if it follows the curve I think it is on.

[PAUSE]

That is the contract for the next thirty pages. Wild but rooted. Speculative but cited. The kind of writing that the rest of the book would not have permitted, and that the closing letter to twenty thirty in Chapter Eighteen would deflate if I tried to put it there.

[PAUSE]

If a reader in twenty thirty-one has bought this book to grade my predictions, this is the chapter where you can grade me most harshly. Some of what follows will look, by then, embarrassingly off. Some of it will look, in the unflattering retrospect that books-on-transitions always get, banal — the obvious thing nobody had quite said yet. The interesting category — the predictions that turn out to be roughly the right shape, badly miscalibrated on timing — is the one I am writing for.

[PAUSE]

Six scenarios. Then the upside I am most hopeful for. Then the downside I am most afraid of. Then a handoff to the letter that closes the book.

[LONG PAUSE]

One. The Petrillo (Peh-trill-oh) settlement actually happens.

[PAUSE]

In May twenty twenty-six, the structural argument of Chapter Six — that the institutional response to the Artificial Intelligence training problem will, on the historical pattern, converge on the levy-and-redistribute mechanism James Caesar Petrillo built in nineteen forty-eight for recorded music — is still, on the page, an argument from analogy. The eighty-eight percent is the political mandate. The GEMA ruling is the legal precedent. The Stability and UMG-style alliances are the commercial templates. The Musical AI (Ay-Eye) creative-weight-attribution work is the technical infrastructure. None of it has yet been assembled into a working settlement.

[PAUSE]

By twenty thirty-one, I think the assembly is done. Not perfectly, not globally, not without exclusions — but done, in at least one major jurisdiction, as a working mechanism that a working creative encounters on a bank statement.

[PAUSE]

The shape of the thing I expect. A national or supranational AI Performance Trust Fund, modelled on the MPTF, capitalised by a statutory per-output levy on commercial generative output, governed by a joint labour-platform body, distributed to working creatives whose training-data contributions were identifiable through the creative-weight-attribution layer described in Chapter Twelve. The first cheques are small. A working illustrator opens a statement and sees the line, AI Royalty Distribution. Forty-three pounds and eighteen pence. A session musician sees one hundred and twenty-seven pounds and forty-two pence. A photographer whose backlist sat in a Getty-class licensed dataset sees a meaningful four-figure annual sum.

[PAUSE]

The cultural significance of those small numbers will, I think, be larger than the numbers themselves. The first cheque is the moment the eighty-eight percent becomes a fact rather than a demand. [SHORT PAUSE] The mechanism is on the books. The architecture is real. The question stops being whether there will be a Petrillo settlement and starts being how much, to whom, on what calibration.

[PAUSE]

[calm] [EMPHASIS] The bargaining ground for the next decade is set on that question.

[LONG PAUSE]

Two. The internet bifurcates, formally.

[PAUSE]

Chapter Four made the case that the web of twenty twenty-six was, in measurable ways, splitting into two distinct attention environments — the Dead Internet of synthetic content optimised for synthetic attention, and the Living Web of provenance-stamped, human-verified work made for an audience that had organised itself around the difference.

[PAUSE]

By twenty thirty-one, I think the bifurcation is no longer a cultural metaphor. I think it is operational infrastructure.

[PAUSE]

The provenance stack the book has spent so many pages cataloguing — C.2.P.A., SynthID, the Cannes Disclosure Standard, the A.P. and BBC wire-service signing chains, the Adobe content credentials — will, by twenty thirty-one, have stitched together into a functioning verification layer. Major browsers will render an indicator. Major platforms will, under pressure from advertisers tired of paying for bot-on-bot impressions, surface verified-human content separately from synthetic. A handful of human-only subscription services — I would not bet against the BBC, The New York Times and one or two of the streaming-music incumbents launching first — will offer "no synthetic content ever" as a paid product.

[PAUSE]

The split internet is not, in the version I expect, a clean split. The synthetic layer will dwarf the verified layer in volume, by perhaps a hundred to one. But the verified layer will capture an outsized share of paid attention, advertiser dollars, and — most importantly — cultural credit. The slop ceiling of Chapter Five, measured at forty-four to three on Deezer in April twenty twenty-six, will by twenty thirty-one have hardened into a roughly stable ratio across most major content categories. Synthetic supply dominant on the upload side, verified-human attention dominant on the consumption side.

[PAUSE]

The Living Web is, by twenty thirty-one, no longer an aspiration. It is a distribution layer you can buy access to, with a different economics from the synthetic layer running underneath it.

[LONG PAUSE]

Three. World models replace flat video as the default high-end medium.

[PAUSE]

Chapter Eight argued that the most important technical shift of twenty twenty-five to twenty twenty-six was not the video models but the world models — Marble (Mar-ble), Genie Three, the Hunyuan Three D family — and that the rate at which spatial-AI tooling was moving from research demos to consumer product was the underappreciated structural story of the period.

[PAUSE]

[excited] By twenty thirty-one, I think we look back on flat twenty-four-frame video the way twenty twenty-six looks back at black-and-white silent cinema. Still made. Still loved. Still occasionally the right medium for the work. But not the default.

[PAUSE]

The default high-end production format, by twenty thirty-one, is a navigable spatial render — a world that the camera moves through in post, that the audience can choose to view from any angle through a Vision-class headset, that an editor can re-cut from a different perspective two months after principal photography is done. The boundary between film, games, immersive and live performance that Chapter Eight described as eroding is, by twenty thirty-one, functionally gone for new productions.

[PAUSE]

The career implications, for working filmmakers, are substantial. The skillset of the director-of-photography fragments into world-curation, lighting-prompt design, and post-spatial composition. The skillset of the editor expands into multi-angle viewer choreography. New roles emerge — spatial continuity supervisor, world dramaturg, perspective director — that have no clean analogue in the twenty twenty-six production pipeline.

[PAUSE]

This is also where I expect the legacy industries' Chapter Seven strategic positioning to come back to haunt them. The studios that bet on flat AI-generated video as the next medium will discover, between twenty twenty-seven and twenty twenty-nine, that they bet on the second-to-last format of the previous era. The studios that built world-model fluency into their pipelines — Sony's documented experiments, the AI-native studios like Imaginae and Critterz, a handful of regional players in Korea, Japan and India — will be the dominant twenty thirty-one producers of the kind of work that uses the medium the way it actually works.

[LONG PAUSE]

Four. The orchestrator becomes a credentialled guild.

[PAUSE]

The orchestrator role — central to Chapter Eleven, referenced through Chapter Thirteen and Chapter Fourteen — was, in twenty twenty-six, a description of an emerging practice. The Sony forty-nine-agent, seventy-two-skill team was the most-cited case study. The working title circulated in studios and agencies but did not yet carry contract weight.

[PAUSE]

By twenty thirty-one, I think the orchestrator is a credentialled profession with collective bargaining power.

[PAUSE]

The guild structure I expect — and this is the most institutionally specific of the six predictions, so the most likely to be wrong on detail — will look something like the IATSE locals that govern below-the-line film labour, or the Writers Guild's apprenticeship and credit system. Senior orchestrators will carry the credential, demonstrate competence in agent stewardship across a defined toolchain, negotiate over rates and over the quantity of agent labour a single orchestrator can supervise. The Writers Guild will, I think, be among the first to formalise the role. SAG-AFTRA (Sag-Aff-trah), Equity and the games-development unions will follow within eighteen months of whoever moves first.

[PAUSE]

The collective-bargaining angle is the part that will, in twenty thirty-one, look most novel and most obviously inevitable in retrospect. If a single human orchestrator can supervise the work of, say, twenty agents — a number that today's productivity research is pointing at, with substantial variance — then the question of who pays whom for the productivity gain is exactly the Petrillo question from earlier in this chapter, applied to the orchestrator's own labour rather than to the underlying training data. The answer the guild will negotiate is a productivity share of the agent-team output, with the orchestrator's individual rate tracking the value of the supervised work, not the volume of the orchestrator's keystrokes.

[PAUSE]

This is one of the more concrete reasons I am cautiously optimistic about the working-creative position over the next five years. The job that emerged in twenty twenty-six — the maker-as-orchestrator — is a job for which collective bargaining is unusually well-suited, because the value being created is unambiguously human in origin and unambiguously measurable in output. Unions exist for exactly this kind of bargaining problem. The Petrillo template, again.

[LONG PAUSE]

Five. An AI-native studio wins the Palme d'Or before Hollywood does.

[PAUSE]

This is the prediction that will, depending on the reader, look either obviously safe or wildly provocative in twenty thirty-one.

[PAUSE]

Chapter Seven catalogued the new AI-native studios — Imaginae, Wonder, Asteria, Obsidian, Critterz, Gossip Goblin — and made the case that their structural advantage over the legacy studios is not technical. It is cultural. They have no calcified rules to unlearn. They have no inherited risk-aversion. They have no franchise-template gravity to pull them back to the engine-optimal move. By the chess-grandmasters analogy in Chapter Fifteen, they are, by default, in a better position to play the move the machine would not have generated.

[PAUSE]

By twenty thirty-one, I think one of them — or, more likely, a successor company emerging from a director who learned in their orbit — wins the Palme d'Or, the Golden Bear, the Silver Lion or one of the other top European festival awards. For its writing or direction. Not for its technology.

[PAUSE]

The cultural moment when that happens will be larger than the prize itself. It will be the Petrillo Settlement of cinema-craft acceptance. The moment the question shifts from, can AI-native cinema be art, to, which AI-native film will be canonical. The follow-on year, I expect the Academy — having spent twenty twenty-five to twenty twenty-eight reinforcing its, you must be human to win, rule for above-the-line crafts — to add a parallel category, or modify the existing one, to recognise hybrid human-orchestrator-AI authorship under contested but workable rules. The festival juries will get there before the Academy does. They almost always do.

[PAUSE]

The corollary, for the legacy studios, is bleak. The diagnosis in Chapter Seven — that Hollywood, commercial music and triple-A games spent fifteen years optimising toward the engine-optimal move and arrived at the AI moment producing exactly the work the engines can now replicate cheapest — will be on full display when the first AI-native Palme winner is, recognisably, not a tentpole. By twenty thirty-one, the Hollywood studio system will be in the position the major-label music industry was in around two thousand and eight. Still dominant by revenue, visibly losing the cultural argument, scrambling for the next operating model.

[LONG PAUSE]

Six. The platform monopoly cracks.

[PAUSE]

[hesitant] The reason I am giving this prediction last among the six is that it is the one I hold most loosely. The structural argument is clean. The timeline is the thing I cannot pin.

[PAUSE]

By twenty thirty-one, I do not think the Open A.I. (Open-Ay-Eye) — Anthropic (An-throp-ic) — Google triad of frontier-AI dominance will look the way it does in May twenty twenty-six. The pressures pushing against it, catalogued in different chapters of this book, are as follows.

[PAUSE]

First. The open-weight ecosystem — Tencent's Hunyuan, Alibaba's Qwen and Wan, DeepSeek, Meta's Llama, Mistral, Stability — collectively used by, per Chapter Sixteen, some eighty percent of startups pitching the major venture funds by spring twenty twenty-six. Open weights compound. Closed weights, by structure, do not.

[PAUSE]

Second. The consent-trained category from the new section in Chapter Six — Adobe Firefly, Bria, Getty's licensed model, Moonvalley Marey, AIODE, the Stability and UMG-style alliances — which, by twenty thirty-one, has either forced the frontier labs to license their training data retroactively, or has displaced them in the enterprise procurement environment by carrying indemnities the frontier labs cannot match.

[PAUSE]

Third. The sovereign-model trend, only just starting in twenty twenty-six, in which national governments — I would put France, Korea, India, the U.A.E., Brazil, Japan on the early list — fund consent-trained, citizen-licensed foundation models as a strategic asset, governed under sovereign rules, distributing royalties through national rights-holder bodies.

[PAUSE]

Fourth. The bifurcating internet from earlier in this chapter, which by twenty thirty-one makes which model trained on what a visible product-marketing claim, in the way fair-trade-certified or organic became visible product-marketing claims in food.

[PAUSE]

By twenty thirty-one, the AI model market I expect looks much more like the twenty twenty-four cloud market than the twenty twenty-five AI market. Multiple major model families. Sovereign options. Open-weight defaults at the long tail. Indemnification and provenance as standard procurement requirements. The frontier-lab valuations of twenty twenty-five either justified by deep enterprise penetration in narrow categories, or remembered the way A-O-L's nineteen ninety-nine valuation is remembered.

[PAUSE]

The corollary I want to put on the page, because it has not yet been said cleanly anywhere else in this book. [SHORT PAUSE] The platform companies dominant in May twenty twenty-six are, on the historical pattern, unlikely to be the platform companies dominant in twenty thirty-one. The history of computing is that the dominant platform of one era is rarely the dominant platform of the next. The Wintel of the nineteen-nineties, the iOS and Android of the twenty-tens, the A.W.S. and Azure of the twenty-twenties — each was a dominant pairing whose successor came from a direction the incumbents did not see. Whatever the dominant generative-AI platform of twenty thirty-one is, I think there is a non-trivial chance it is a company most readers of this book — including me — have not yet heard of.

[LONG PAUSE]

The upside I am most hopeful for. The Dream Machine becomes literal.

[PAUSE]

I want to spend the rest of this chapter on two scenarios that do not fit cleanly into the predict-and-date register, because they are the ones I think about most when I am away from the work and most off-script when I am in it.

[PAUSE]

The first is what happens if the title of this book turns out to be a literal description.

[PAUSE]

The phrase Dream Machine, as I have used it through the newsletter and the book, has been a metaphor for the apparatus of generative AI. A composite name for the platforms, models, infrastructure and human labour that, between them, produce the new creative work.

[PAUSE]

I want to entertain the possibility that, by twenty thirty-one, the phrase describes a literal piece of consumer hardware — a wearable creative-cognition prosthetic, sitting somewhere on the continuum between a Vision Pro and a brain-computer interface, that watches a working creative make work, learns their taste, infers their intent, and contributes — in real time, in collaboration, at the speed of thought — to the work in progress.

[PAUSE]

The technical building blocks are visible. Apple's Vision Pro and the next-generation headset class are mature consumer products by twenty thirty-one. The brain-computer interface category, dominated in twenty twenty-six by medical applications, is on a trajectory the consumer hardware press has been undercounting. The agentic orchestration layer that I described in Chapter Eleven is, by twenty thirty-one, no longer something a human types instructions into. It is something a human converses with, gestures to, thinks at. The interaction model of prompting is, by twenty thirty-one, an obsolete U.X. pattern, remembered the way command-line interfaces are remembered now.

[PAUSE]

The literal Dream Machine, in this scenario, is not a separate device. It is the orchestration layer made embodied. A creative cognition prosthetic that allows a working creative to externalise, manipulate, sketch, iterate and finish creative work at a fluency that is, today, available only to the smallest set of professionals who have spent thirty years building the relevant neural pathways. The leverage the device provides is not replacement of the human imagination. It is the closing of the gap between the human imagination and what the human hand can, today, get out into the world.

[PAUSE]

If this device — or one like it — ships at consumer price in the period this chapter covers, the access principle from Chapter Fifteen gets a lift I cannot easily exaggerate. The bedroom-Hollywood dynamic from Chapter Fourteen — the teenager with a midrange G.P.U. producing studio-quality output — extends to every discipline, including the ones that today still require expensive equipment and decades of haptic training. The new geography I argued for in Chapter Fifteen — the dispersion of canonical creative work to places the previous century forgot — becomes structurally inevitable rather than aspirational.

[PAUSE]

[EMPHASIS] The Dream Machine, in this scenario, is not the platforms. It is the prosthetic. The platforms are infrastructure underneath it. The dreaming is, finally, located where the title was always insisting it was located. In the human at the centre of the apparatus.

[PAUSE]

I want this scenario to be the one that comes true. I think there is a real chance — not a high probability, but a meaningfully non-zero one — that it does.

[LONG PAUSE]

The downside I am most afraid of. The audience becomes synthetic.

[PAUSE]

The corresponding worst case I want to put on the page is the failure mode the slop ceiling cannot, by construction, defend against.

[PAUSE]

The slop ceiling, as I described it in Chapter Five, works because the audience can tell — at the speed of a swipe, in the aggregate, with reliability — that synthetic content does not carry the human signal real human attention is calibrated to. The ratio holds, the forty-four to three holds, the audience underweights the slop, because there is an audience, and the audience is made of humans, and the humans are still, in May twenty twenty-six, paying attention to one another.

[PAUSE]

The downside scenario is that, by twenty thirty-one, that last condition has decayed.

[PAUSE]

The mechanism is the one the Dutch researchers cited in Chapter Five and the bot-traffic investigations cited in Chapter Four were already, by spring twenty twenty-six, beginning to document. As synthetic content production becomes free and synthetic attention-allocation agents become widely deployed, the measured audience for any given piece of content drifts further from the human audience. Platforms optimise against the measured audience, because that is what their analytics surface. The content the platforms surface, in turn, becomes the content the synthetic audience underweights least. The synthetic audience grows. The human audience, exhausted, exits. The platforms continue to grow on the measured numbers because the measured numbers are increasingly bot-on-bot. The cultural production layer continues to produce, but produces for bots watching bots. The work loses meaning slowly, then quickly, because the audience that produced meaning has stopped being part of the loop.

[PAUSE]

This is the version of twenty thirty-one I am most afraid of. Not because AI replaces creators. Creators are not the load-bearing structure. [SHORT PAUSE] The audience is the load-bearing structure. And the audience is the thing the architecture of the synthetic internet, deployed without the verification layer described in scenario two of this chapter, is structurally positioned to dissolve.

[PAUSE]

The corresponding fight, the one I think the next five years has to win to avoid the downside, is the audience verification fight. The C.2.P.A.-equivalent for who is watching, not just for who made. The mechanism that lets a creator know — and lets an advertiser pay for, and lets a platform surface — the work that real humans were really paying attention to. The early signals in this direction are there, in the form of platform attestation experiments and the bot-traffic regulatory pressure documented in Chapter Four. None of them are, in May twenty twenty-six, anywhere near the scale of the upstream provenance work.

[PAUSE]

If the next five years build the verification layer for production but not for consumption, the slop ceiling holds in the short term and collapses in the medium term. The creators win the upstream battle. The audience disappears anyway. The downstream meaning of the work, by twenty thirty-one, is gone — not because human creativity has been replaced, but because the social fact that creativity is made for somebody has been quietly dissolved underneath the work.

[PAUSE]

This is the failure mode I think we are most exposed to and least talking about.

[LONG PAUSE]

What we make of it.

[PAUSE]

I want to close this chapter the same way I open the next.

[PAUSE]

The phrase Dream Machine was, when I named the newsletter, a placeholder for a feeling. By the end of Chapter Fifteen it had become an argument. That the apparatus we are building amplifies, multiplies and distributes the dreaming of the humans who direct it, and that the question every working chapter of this book has been about is whose dreams the machine amplifies.

[PAUSE]

What this chapter has tried to do is take that argument out to five years and let it run.

[PAUSE]

Six things I think happen. The Petrillo settlement, made real, on a working creative's bank statement. The internet, formally split. World models, displacing flat video as the default. The orchestrator, credentialled and bargained-for. The first AI-native Palme d'Or. The platform monopoly, cracked. One upside I am hopeful for — the Dream Machine made literal, a creative cognition prosthetic that closes the gap between human imagination and finished work. One downside I am afraid of — the audience layer collapsing under synthetic capture, leaving the work without anyone to make it for.

[PAUSE]

Three of the six will, almost certainly, be wrong. One of them will be wrong in the direction of, I undersold this. One of them will be wrong in the direction of, the timeline was slower than I thought. One of them will be wrong in the direction of, the thing I described did not happen because the thing that happened instead was weirder and more interesting. I do not know yet which is which.

[PAUSE]

The point of the exercise is not to be right about the six. The point is to put a shape on the page, in twenty twenty-six, for the conversation that the working creatives, the studios, the unions, the policy people and the audience will be having with each other, week by week, between now and twenty thirty-one. The four principles from Chapter Fifteen — agency, attribution, access, audience — are the test you apply to each daily decision. The scenarios in this chapter are the direction the aggregate of those decisions is, on my read of the available evidence, most likely pointing in.

[PAUSE]

The title of this book, I have come to think, was always slightly mis-named. The Dream Machine is not the apparatus the platforms built. It is not even the prosthetic the consumer-hardware companies might ship by twenty thirty-one. The Dream Machine is the whole system — the platforms, the prosthetics, the working creatives, the audience, the unions, the institutions, the regulators, the literacy initiatives, the newsletters, the festivals, the awards, the lawsuits and the daily decisions of millions of people about whose work to make and which work to pay attention to. The thing the title points at is the coupled human-machine cultural production system that the period this book covers brought into existence.

[PAUSE]

What we make of that system, between May twenty twenty-six and the moment a reader picks this book up in twenty thirty-one to grade me, is the work.

[PAUSE]

The letter in the next chapter is what I want to leave that reader with.

[PAUSE]

Welcome to the Dream Machine.

[LONG PAUSE]

END OF CHAPTER

[LONG PAUSE]


EPILOGUE

A Letter from the Dream Machine.

[PAUSE]

To the creative person reading this in twenty thirty, picking the book up out of curiosity or for a class or because someone older than you said you should.

[PAUSE]

[quietly] I want to write you a short letter, because by the time you are reading this, more than half of what is in this book will already be wrong.

[PAUSE]

Some of the tools I have spent chapters discussing will be obsolete. Some of the companies will have been bought, broken up or sunk. Some of the law I described as new and contested will have settled into precedent that everyone takes for granted. Some of the people I quoted as authorities will have changed their positions, and some of the predictions I made — the ones I let myself make — will have aged in ways I would now find embarrassing to read back.

[PAUSE]

This is fine. It is, I think, the right outcome for a book written in the moment that this one was written in. The job of a book about a transition is not to predict where the transition lands. The job is to describe the moment honestly — what was being argued about, by whom, with what evidence, and what was at stake — so that the reader picking it up later has, at minimum, an accurate record of what the people in the room thought they were doing.

[PAUSE]

There are a few things I want to flag for you, looking back from your time at mine.

[LONG PAUSE]

What we got right.

[PAUSE]

We got the slop ceiling right. I am almost certain of this, even sitting here in May twenty twenty-six, because the underlying mechanism — that audiences allocate attention to meaning, not to quantity — is one of the most reliable findings in the history of cultural production, and there is no plausible scenario in which it stops working.

[PAUSE]

If you are reading this in twenty thirty and there is a thriving creative economy, it is because the slop ceiling held. The audience that turned away from synthetic content in twenty twenty-six either kept turning away, or the platforms and the creators figured out how to make Artificial Intelligence-augmented work that genuinely earned attention — sincere, transparent, made with care. Both outcomes preserve the underlying contract between maker and audience. Either way, the ratio between volume and attention is still doing its work.

[PAUSE]

If, on the other hand, you are reading this in twenty thirty and the creative economy looks bleaker than the one I have described, it is — almost certainly — because the architecture of the internet was allowed to drift further in the direction the Dutch researchers identified in twenty twenty-five. An attention market optimised against meaning, where the ratio between volume and attention stopped functioning because the audience itself had become indistinguishable from the bots.

[PAUSE]

[calm] [EMPHASIS] The slop ceiling was real. The question for your decade is whether you protected the audience that produced it.

[LONG PAUSE]

What we got wrong.

[PAUSE]

We got the timeline wrong, in both directions.

[PAUSE]

In some places, we expected the change to be slower than it turned out to be. The pace at which world models moved from research demos to consumer features in the eight months I documented in this book was something almost nobody — including me — would have predicted in late twenty twenty-four.

[PAUSE]

In other places, we expected the change to be faster. The full AI (Ay-Eye)-native film studio that was supposed to replace Hollywood by twenty twenty-seven is still, in the spring of twenty twenty-six, mostly a series of well-funded prototypes. The fully autonomous game-development pipeline is, even at Sony, still an assisted pipeline rather than an autonomous one. The headline futures the platform companies have been selling are, in the main, still further off than the press cycle suggested.

[PAUSE]

If you are reading this in twenty thirty, I suspect that the timeline gap will look in retrospect like the most obvious thing about the moment we were in. The interesting changes were the ones nobody put on a slide. The boring changes — the slow institutional repositioning, the contract-by-contract renegotiation of how creators are paid, the patient construction of the C.2.P.A. standards, the union-by-union recalibration of what counts as a fair use of a performer's likeness — were the ones that actually shaped your decade.

[PAUSE]

This is, I think, almost always true of technology transitions. The press loves the visible. The structural change happens in the unvisible. The fact that you can pick this book up in twenty thirty and read it without unusual cost or effort is the result of a thousand small infrastructure decisions, made by people whose names did not appear in this book, that none of us thought to celebrate at the time.

[LONG PAUSE]

What I want you to know about us.

[PAUSE]

[quietly] We were, in twenty twenty-six, frightened. Most of the working creatives I knew that year were carrying a layer of low-grade fear — about their livelihoods, about their crafts, about the institutions that had organised their working lives — that I do not think showed up in the trade press or the platform keynotes or even in this book as much as it should have.

[PAUSE]

We were also, I think, more hopeful than we said out loud. Most of the working creatives I knew that year were also doing some of the most experimental, most curious, most adventurous work of their careers, because the tools made things possible that had been impossible the year before and they wanted to find out what.

[PAUSE]

Both states were true at the same time. They will be true again in your decade, about whatever the next transition is. I don't think we managed the fear well. I think we did okay with the hope. I am proud of the people who turned up, week after week, to argue about how all of this should go.

[LONG PAUSE]

A specific request.

[PAUSE]

If the institutions of the generative creative economy — the unions, the rights bodies, the consultations, the literacy initiatives, the disclosure standards, the festivals, the regional studios, the indie filmmakers in places that the previous century forgot — are still doing their work when you read this, [SHORT PAUSE] thank the people who built them. Most of those people have not become rich, or famous, or particularly comfortable. They were working creatives, union officers, policy officers, festival organisers, technologists, lawyers, professors and freelancers who chose, in their unglamorous moments, to do the slow institutional work that this kind of economy needs.

[PAUSE]

The platform companies will not remember them. The audience will not know their names. The cultural histories will record the names of the directors and the songwriters and the celebrity AI controversies. The institutional work happens in the basement and the archive and the union office and the policy briefing.

[PAUSE]

[calm] [EMPHASIS] If you are inheriting a creative economy that works, you are inheriting it from those people. Thank them while they are still around to be thanked.

[LONG PAUSE]

A specific request to working creatives in twenty thirty.

[PAUSE]

Keep going.

[PAUSE]

The pace of change will be at least as fast in your decade as it was in mine. The next paradigm of AI tooling — whatever it is — will, I am sure, make the world models and agents of twenty twenty-six look as quaint as Photoshop in nineteen ninety-six. You will have your own version of the day Sora (Sore-ah) landed. You will have your own Tilly Norwood week. You will have your own eighty-eight percent. You will have your own choice between the extractive and the generative economy.

[PAUSE]

Whatever the new tools are, the principles I tried to articulate in Chapter Fifteen — agency, attribution, access, audience — will, I am confident, still apply. They are not specific to the twenty twenty-six transition. They are general properties of how a humane creative economy organises itself, and they will work in any technology environment that produces creative outputs at scale.

[PAUSE]

The deeper conviction underneath the principles will, I think, also still apply. The age of the How — the long century in which the central question of working creative life was, can you do the thing? — was, by twenty twenty-six, visibly ending. The age of the Why — taste, intent, authenticity, the willingness to take a risk on the move the data does not endorse — was visibly starting. The image I borrowed from elite chess in Chapter Fifteen, of grandmasters winning by deliberately playing the move the engine would not have played, will still be the picture I would commend to you. By the time you read this, the engines will be better. The pressure to play the engine's optimal line will be stronger. The competitive advantage of the deliberately un-machine-like move — of the move that is yours because no machine, trained on what came before, could have generated it — will, if anything, have widened.

[PAUSE]

Apply them. Argue for them. Build for them. Defend them when they are under pressure.

[PAUSE]

And — this is the part I want you to take seriously even when it feels grandiose — write the newsletter. Whatever your version of the newsletter is. The thing that, when the moment comes, you sit down on a Monday morning and decide to write because nobody else seems to be doing it. Most of those newsletters won't matter. A few of them will. The one you are reading was, for me, one of the most consequential decisions I ever accidentally made.

[PAUSE]

You will know which moment is the moment, when it arrives. You will know because the people you respect will be staring at the same news cycle, looking at each other in the same way, asking the same question.

[PAUSE]

[EMPHASIS] When that moment arrives in your decade, write the newsletter.

[LONG PAUSE]

Closing.

[PAUSE]

I want to end this letter the same way I have ended every issue of Dream Machine for the past six months, because the line has come to mean more to me than I expected it to.

[PAUSE]

If you've got any recommendations or things we need to know about for our next edition, please feel free to reach out.

[PAUSE]

That sentence was, for all the time the newsletter has run, my way of acknowledging that I did not have the picture on my own. That the readers were the network. That the work was a conversation, not a broadcast. The newsletter has only ever been as good as the community of creatives, technologists, union reps, academics, festival programmers, indie filmmakers, working musicians and audience members who have, week after week, sent me the things I would otherwise have missed.

[PAUSE]

If you are reading this in twenty thirty, the newsletter is still — in whatever form it has taken by then — that same conversation. Keep adding to it. The Dream Machine, in the end, is not the AI. It is the network of humans who, between them, are deciding what the machine is for.

[PAUSE]

[calm] Welcome to the Dream Machine.

[PAUSE]

Pete Woodbridge. DreamLab, the North West, May twenty twenty-six.

[LONG PAUSE]

END OF EPILOGUE

[LONG PAUSE]
